========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Well, what finally pushed me to come in is... I guess it’s mostly affecting my work and some important personal stuff. I’m a software developer, and lately I just keep getting up during sprint planning. Which, to be fair, our scrum master talks so incredibly slow, it’s like pulling teeth. I used to work at this startup a few years ago where we did our standups literally standing up, and it was great because it took five minutes tops. But now everything is on Zoom, and I even bought this ridiculously expensive ergonomic chair thinking it would make a difference for my posture, but... anyway, sorry. 

The point is, even when I know I absolutely should stay put, I keep leaving my seat. It happens during really important things too, not just work. Like, I was on a mortgage call the other day. We're trying to refinance right now—my husband actually thinks we should wait to see if rates drop more, but I just want to get it over with—and I’ll be on the phone with the broker, knowing I need to pay attention, and suddenly I just decide I desperately need a glass of water. Or I'll just get up to check something in the other room right that second. It's just getting really ridiculous, so I figured I should finally talk to someone about it.

It's... well, it's definitely not anxiety. I don't feel nervous or anything. It's more like this physical thing. It's definitely not a new thing. Honestly, it goes way back. I remember in elementary school, teachers were constantly moving my desk around. They were always reminding me to stay seated because I was just always popping up out of my chair. Or I'd blurt out answers before they even called on me. But now everything is on Zoom, and I even bought this ridiculously expensive ergonomic chair thinking it would make a difference for my posture, but... anyway, sorry. \n\nThe point is, even when I know I absolutely should stay put, I keep leaving my seat. It happens during really important things too, not just work. Like, I was on a mortgage call the other day. We're trying to refinance right now—my husband actually thinks we should wait to see if rates drop more, but I just want to get it over with—and I’ll be on the phone with the broker, knowing I need to pay attention, and suddenly I just decide I desperately need a glass of water. Or I'll just get up to check something in the other room right that second. It's just getting really ridiculous, so I figured I should finally talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you get up from your seat during these situations, does it feel like you physically can't sit still—like there's restless energy you need to burn off—or does it feel more like anxiety or an urgent need to do something specific?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's definitely not anxiety. I don't feel nervous or anything. It's more like this physical thing. Even if I'm sitting there and I look totally calm on the outside, inside there's this pressure building up. Like I just need to move, or switch what I'm doing, or at least have something in my hands to fiddle with, or I'm going to crawl out of my skin. \n\nActually, I started keeping one of those Rubik's cubes on my desk to help with that. My brother got it for me for Christmas—he's always getting me these little puzzle things because he knows I like them. He's an engineer too, but civil, not software. We used to build these massive Lego sets together when we were kids. Our parents had this huge basement and we'd just take over the whole floor. But yeah, the Rubik's cube helps a little, I guess, but mostly I just feel that weird internal pressure until I finally give up and walk around.\n\nDoctor: How far back does this feeling of needing to move around and not being able to sit still go? Has it been something you've dealt with your whole life, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not a new thing. Honestly, it goes way back. I remember in elementary school, teachers were constantly moving my desk around. They were always reminding me to stay seated because I was just always popping up out of my chair. Or I'd blurt out answers before they even called on me. Which is funny, because my daughter does the exact same thing now in her third-grade class. Her teacher, Mrs. Gable, is so sweet about it, though. She has this whole point system with stickers, which I think is a great idea. We tried doing a sticker chart at home for chores, but I kept forgetting to buy the stickers, and then the dog actually ate half the chart. He's a Golden Retriever mix, total goofball, we rescued him about three years ago. \n\nBut yeah, back in school, I was always rushing through my work, even when I totally understood it. I just wanted to be done so I could go do something else. So I guess this whole not-being-able-to-sit-still thing has been with me pretty much my whole life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My brother got it for me for Christmas—he's always getting me these little puzzle things because he knows I like them. He's an engineer too, but civil, not software. We used to build these massive Lego sets together when we were kids. Our parents had this huge basement and we'd just take over the whole floor. But yeah, the Rubik's cube helps a little, I guess, but mostly I just feel that weird internal pressure until I finally give up and walk around.\n\nDoctor: How far back does this feeling of needing to move around and not being able to sit still go? Has it been something you've dealt with your whole life, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not a new thing. Honestly, it goes way back. I remember in elementary school, teachers were constantly moving my desk around. They were always reminding me to stay seated because I was just always popping up out of my chair. Or I'd blurt out answers before they even called on me. Which is funny, because my daughter does the exact same thing now in her third-grade class. Her teacher, Mrs. Gable, is so sweet about it, though. She has this whole point system with stickers, which I think is a great idea. We tried doing a sticker chart at home for chores, but I kept forgetting to buy the stickers, and then the dog actually ate half the chart. He's a Golden Retriever mix, total goofball, we rescued him about three years ago. \n\nBut yeah, back in school, I was always rushing through my work, even when I totally understood it. I just wanted to be done so I could go do something else. So I guess this whole not-being-able-to-sit-still thing has been with me pretty much my whole life.\n\nDoctor: When you were rushing through your schoolwork back then, or even now at work, do you find yourself making careless mistakes because you're going too fast, or do you actually have trouble focusing and keeping track of what you're supposed to be doing in the first place?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's not so much the careless mistakes, I don't think. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I remembered to switch the laundry, or if we need to buy more dog food, or... oh! I was actually thinking the other day about how I need to call my mom because her birthday is coming up next week. She's turning 68 and she really wants to go to this Italian place downtown, but parking there is just an absolute nightmare. It's like... I try to pull myself back. I really do. I'll shake my head, blink a few times, and tell myself, "Okay, focus on the code." And I can usually force myself to read the next line or two. Which, to be fair, our scrum master talks so incredibly slow, it’s like pulling teeth. I used to work at this startup a few years ago where we did our standups literally standing up, and it was great because it took five minutes tops. But now everything is on Zoom, and I even bought this ridiculously expensive ergonomic chair thinking it would make a difference for my posture, but... anyway, sorry. \n\nThe point is, even when I know I absolutely should stay put, I keep leaving my seat. Or I'll just get up to check something in the other room right that second. It's just getting really ridiculous, so I figured I should finally talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you get up from your seat during these situations, does it feel like you physically can't sit still—like there's restless energy you need to burn off—or does it feel more like anxiety or an urgent need to do something specific?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's definitely not anxiety. I don't feel nervous or anything. It's more like this physical thing. Even if I'm sitting there and I look totally calm on the outside, inside there's this pressure building up. My brother got it for me for Christmas—he's always getting me these little puzzle things because he knows I like them. He's an engineer too, but civil, not software. We used to build these massive Lego sets together when we were kids. Our parents had this huge basement and we'd just take over the whole floor. But yeah, the Rubik's cube helps a little, I guess, but mostly I just feel that weird internal pressure until I finally give up and walk around.\n\nDoctor: How far back does this feeling of needing to move around and not being able to sit still go? Has it been something you've dealt with your whole life, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not a new thing. Honestly, it goes way back. I remember in elementary school, teachers were constantly moving my desk around. They were always reminding me to stay seated because I was just always popping up out of my chair. Or I'd blurt out answers before they even called on me. Which is funny, because my daughter does the exact same thing now in her third-grade class. Her teacher, Mrs. Gable, is so sweet about it, though. She has this whole point system with stickers, which I think is a great idea. We tried doing a sticker chart at home for chores, but I kept forgetting to buy the stickers, and then the dog actually ate half the chart. He's a Golden Retriever mix, total goofball, we rescued him about three years ago. \n\nBut yeah, back in school, I was always rushing through my work, even when I totally understood it. I just wanted to be done so I could go do something else. I'll sit down, totally meaning to read through it all properly. But then, maybe five minutes in, I'll realize I've just been staring at the exact same paragraph the whole time. \n\nMy brain will just be off somewhere else completely. I start out fine, but then my mind is just suddenly juggling five completely unrelated things instead of looking at the code in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders like that during the pull request review, are you able to redirect yourself back to focus on the task if you really try, or does it feel almost impossible to pull your attention back no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: It's like... I try to pull myself back. I really do. I'll shake my head, blink a few times, and tell myself, \"Okay, focus on the code.\" And I can usually force myself to read the next line or two. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's... I think it's mostly automatic? Like, I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time. In meetings, I'm almost always bouncing my knee, or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair constantly. I really don't notice how much I'm doing it until someone looks over at me, you know? Like my manager, Dave. He has this very specific look he gives me when my pen clicking gets too loud. 

Dave is actually leaving the company next month, which is a whole thing. He's moving to Austin. Everyone seems to be moving to Austin lately! My cousin moved there two years ago and she says the food scene is amazing, especially the tacos. I love a good breakfast taco. We tried to make them at home last weekend, but I accidentally bought corn tortillas instead of flour, and they just completely fell apart when I tried to fold them. My husband said it was fine and we could just eat them like a scramble, but it wasn't the same. I really need to remember to check the labels more carefully when I'm at the grocery store. The lighting in there always gives me a headache anyway, so I just try to rush through... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the fidgeting. Yeah, I don't really think about doing it, my body just sort of does it on its own until someone points it out.

Eventually, it just pops back up. \n\nSo yeah, I'll start with all these good intentions, totally meaning to get through the pull request or those mortgage papers, and then... boom. Five minutes later, I'm staring at the exact same section, completely zoned out, thinking about... I don't know, whether I should repaint the guest bathroom. We painted it this sort of seafoam green when we moved in, but now I'm thinking maybe a warm terracotta would be better? My sister just did her kitchen in terracotta and it looks amazing. She has all these copper pots hanging up, which I love, but honestly, who has the time to keep copper polished? I can barely keep up with the regular dishes. My husband is supposed to unload the dishwasher in the mornings, but he always leaves the Tupperware because he says it's still wet. Which, to be fair, it usually is. But anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Trying to focus. It's just really, really hard to keep my brain on track once it decides to wander off.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in those meetings or trying to focus on work, is the urge to get up and move around happening most of the time, or is it something that mainly kicks in when you're trying to concentrate on something boring or difficult?\n\nPatient: It's honestly... it's pretty much all the time. Like, even if I'm just sitting there, looking perfectly calm on the outside—like during one of those endless all-hands meetings we have on Thursdays. They cater lunch for those sometimes, which is nice. Last week they brought in sandwiches from that deli down on 4th Street, but they completely forgot the vegetarian options, so my coworker Sarah was so mad. She's been a strict vegetarian for like, ten years, ever since she took this trip to India. I've always wanted to go to India. The colors and the architecture just look incredible in pictures. \n\nBut yeah, during those meetings, even if I look fine, inside I feel this intense pressure. Like I absolutely need to move, or switch tasks, or just do something with my hands, or else I'm going to crawl out of my own skin. I usually end up shredding my napkins or clicking my pen until someone glares at me. I actually bought one of those fidget rings online to try and be more subtle about it. It was supposed to arrive yesterday, but the delivery guy left it at the wrong house. My neighbor down the street brought it over this morning—he's a retired pilot, always wears these really cool vintage aviator jackets... wait, sorry, what was the question again? Oh, the urge to move. Yeah, it's just this constant internal buzzing, really, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're fidgeting with things like the Rubik's cube or shredding napkins, does it feel like you're doing it to help yourself calm down or regulate your energy, or do you find yourself doing the same repetitive movements over and over again almost automatically, even if you're not really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It's... I think it's mostly automatic? Like, I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time. In meetings, I'm almost always bouncing my knee, or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair constantly. I really don't notice how much I'm doing it until someone looks over at me, you know? Like my manager, Dave. He has this very specific look he gives me when my pen clicking gets too loud. \n\nDave is actually leaving the company next month, which is a whole thing. He's moving to Austin. Everyone seems to be moving to Austin lately! My cousin moved there two years ago and she says the food scene is amazing, especially the tacos. I love a good breakfast taco. We tried to make them at home last weekend, but I accidentally bought corn tortillas instead of flour, and they just completely fell apart when I tried to fold them. My husband said it was fine and we could just eat them like a scramble, but it wasn't the same. I really need to remember to check the labels more carefully when I'm at the grocery store. The lighting in there always gives me a headache anyway, so I just try to rush through... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the fidgeting. Yeah, I don't really think about doing it, my body just sort of does it on its own until someone points it out.\n\n[Last Question]\nDespite all the fidgeting, difficulty focusing, and getting up from your seat, how are you actually doing with your work performance overall? Are you meeting your deadlines, getting good reviews, or is this stuff actually causing real problems for your job?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's definitely starting to cause some issues. Like, it's creating friction, you know? At work and even at home. People are having to follow up with me a lot more than they used to. My manager had to ping me three times last week about a status report. And I hate that, because I don't want to be *that* person. I worry I'm becoming the unreliable one, especially when it comes to planning tasks or organizing things, even though I really am trying so hard. 

It's just like with planning our family vacation this summer. I was supposed to book the Airbnb in Florida, but I kept putting it off because I couldn't decide which neighborhood was best, and then I started researching the history of Key West, which is actually super fascinating. Did you know Ernest Hemingway had six-toed cats there? And they still have descendants living at his house! I love cats. We have two, a tabby named Waffles and a black cat named Salem. Salem is a total menace, he keeps knocking my water glass off the nightstand. I had to buy a plastic tumbler just so I wouldn't have to keep vacuuming up broken glass at 3 AM. 

But yeah, because I got distracted with all that, by the time I went to book the house, all the good ones were taken, and my husband was really frustrated. So, it's just... I'm trying, but the follow-ups and the dropped balls are definitely becoming a real problem.

He's a Golden Retriever mix, total goofball, we rescued him about three years ago. \n\nBut yeah, back in school, I was always rushing through my work, even when I totally understood it. I just wanted to be done so I could go do something else. So I guess this whole not-being-able-to-sit-still thing has been with me pretty much my whole life.\n\nDoctor: When you were rushing through your schoolwork back then, or even now at work, do you find yourself making careless mistakes because you're going too fast, or do you actually have trouble focusing and keeping track of what you're supposed to be doing in the first place?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's not so much the careless mistakes, I don't think. I mean, I'm pretty good at my job. But it's more like... okay, so say I need to review a pull request for one of the junior devs, right? Or even those mortgage documents I mentioned earlier. Last time we went, we had to park like four blocks away in the pouring rain, and my husband ruined his favorite suede shoes. But anyway, yeah, I just... I start out fine, but then my mind is just suddenly juggling five completely unrelated things instead of looking at the code in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders like that during the pull request review, are you able to redirect yourself back to focus on the task if you really try, or does it feel almost impossible to pull your attention back no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: It's like... I try to pull myself back. I really do. I'll shake my head, blink a few times, and tell myself, \"Okay, focus on the code.\" And I can usually force myself to read the next line or two. We're trying to refinance right now—my husband actually thinks we should wait to see if rates drop more, but I just want to get it over with—and I’ll be on the phone with the broker, knowing I need to pay attention, and suddenly I just decide I desperately need a glass of water. Or I'll just get up to check something in the other room right that second. It's just getting really ridiculous, so I figured I should finally talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you get up from your seat during these situations, does it feel like you physically can't sit still—like there's restless energy you need to burn off—or does it feel more like anxiety or an urgent need to do something specific?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's definitely not anxiety. I don't feel nervous or anything. It's more like this physical thing. Even if I'm sitting there and I look totally calm on the outside, inside there's this pressure building up. Like I just need to move, or switch what I'm doing, or at least have something in my hands to fiddle with, or I'm going to crawl out of my skin. \n\nActually, I started keeping one of those Rubik's cubes on my desk to help with that. My brother got it for me for Christmas—he's always getting me these little puzzle things because he knows I like them. He's an engineer too, but civil, not software. We used to build these massive Lego sets together when we were kids. Our parents had this huge basement and we'd just take over the whole floor. But yeah, the Rubik's cube helps a little, I guess, but mostly I just feel that weird internal pressure until I finally give up and walk around.\n\nDoctor: How far back does this feeling of needing to move around and not being able to sit still go? Has it been something you've dealt with your whole life, or is this something that's gotten worse more recently?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not a new thing. Honestly, it goes way back. I remember in elementary school, teachers were constantly moving my desk around. They were always reminding me to stay seated because I was just always popping up out of my chair. Or I'd blurt out answers before they even called on me. Which is funny, because my daughter does the exact same thing now in her third-grade class. Her teacher, Mrs. Gable, is so sweet about it, though. She has this whole point system with stickers, which I think is a great idea. We tried doing a sticker chart at home for chores, but I kept forgetting to buy the stickers, and then the dog actually ate half the chart. He's a Golden Retriever mix, total goofball, we rescued him about three years ago. \n\nBut yeah, back in school, I was always rushing through my work, even when I totally understood it. I just wanted to be done so I could go do something else. So I guess this whole not-being-able-to-sit-still thing has been with me pretty much my whole life.\n\nDoctor: When you were rushing through your schoolwork back then, or even now at work, do you find yourself making careless mistakes because you're going too fast, or do you actually have trouble focusing and keeping track of what you're supposed to be doing in the first place?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's not so much the careless mistakes, I don't think. I mean, I'm pretty good at my job. But it's more like... okay, so say I need to review a pull request for one of the junior devs, right? Or even those mortgage documents I mentioned earlier. I'll sit down, totally meaning to read through it all properly. But then, maybe five minutes in, I'll realize I've just been staring at the exact same paragraph the whole time. \n\nMy brain will just be off somewhere else completely. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I remembered to switch the laundry, or if we need to buy more dog food, or... oh! I was actually thinking the other day about how I need to call my mom because her birthday is coming up next week. She's turning 68 and she really wants to go to this Italian place downtown, but parking there is just an absolute nightmare. Last time we went, we had to park like four blocks away in the pouring rain, and my husband ruined his favorite suede shoes. But anyway, yeah, I just... I start out fine, but then my mind is just suddenly juggling five completely unrelated things instead of looking at the code in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders like that during the pull request review, are you able to redirect yourself back to focus on the task if you really try, or does it feel almost impossible to pull your attention back no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: It's like... I try to pull myself back. I really do. I'll shake my head, blink a few times, and tell myself, \"Okay, focus on the code.\" And I can usually force myself to read the next line or two. But it's exhausting. It feels like trying to hold a beach ball underwater. Eventually, it just pops back up. \n\nSo yeah, I'll start with all these good intentions, totally meaning to get through the pull request or those mortgage papers, and then... boom. Five minutes later, I'm staring at the exact same section, completely zoned out, thinking about... I don't know, whether I should repaint the guest bathroom. We painted it this sort of seafoam green when we moved in, but now I'm thinking maybe a warm terracotta would be better? My sister just did her kitchen in terracotta and it looks amazing. She has all these copper pots hanging up, which I love, but honestly, who has the time to keep copper polished? I can barely keep up with the regular dishes. My husband is supposed to unload the dishwasher in the mornings, but he always leaves the Tupperware because he says it's still wet. Which, to be fair, it usually is. But anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Trying to focus. It's just really, really hard to keep my brain on track once it decides to wander off.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in those meetings or trying to focus on work, is the urge to get up and move around happening most of the time, or is it something that mainly kicks in when you're trying to concentrate on something boring or difficult?\n\nPatient: It's honestly... it's pretty much all the time. Like, even if I'm just sitting there, looking perfectly calm on the outside—like during one of those endless all-hands meetings we have on Thursdays. They cater lunch for those sometimes, which is nice. Last week they brought in sandwiches from that deli down on 4th Street, but they completely forgot the vegetarian options, so my coworker Sarah was so mad. She's been a strict vegetarian for like, ten years, ever since she took this trip to India. I've always wanted to go to India. The colors and the architecture just look incredible in pictures. \n\nBut yeah, during those meetings, even if I look fine, inside I feel this intense pressure. Like I absolutely need to move, or switch tasks, or just do something with my hands, or else I'm going to crawl out of my own skin. I usually end up shredding my napkins or clicking my pen until someone glares at me. I actually bought one of those fidget rings online to try and be more subtle about it. It was supposed to arrive yesterday, but the delivery guy left it at the wrong house. My neighbor down the street brought it over this morning—he's a retired pilot, always wears these really cool vintage aviator jackets... wait, sorry, what was the question again? Oh, the urge to move. Yeah, it's just this constant internal buzzing, really, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're fidgeting with things like the Rubik's cube or shredding napkins, does it feel like you're doing it to help yourself calm down or regulate your energy, or do you find yourself doing the same repetitive movements over and over again almost automatically, even if you're not really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It's... I think it's mostly automatic? Like, I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time. In meetings, I'm almost always bouncing my knee, or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair constantly. I really don't notice how much I'm doing it until someone looks over at me, you know? Like my manager, Dave. He has this very specific look he gives me when my pen clicking gets too loud. \n\nDave is actually leaving the company next month, which is a whole thing. He's moving to Austin. Everyone seems to be moving to Austin lately! My cousin moved there two years ago and she says the food scene is amazing, especially the tacos. I love a good breakfast taco. We tried to make them at home last weekend, but I accidentally bought corn tortillas instead of flour, and they just completely fell apart when I tried to fold them. My husband said it was fine and we could just eat them like a scramble, but it wasn't the same. I really need to remember to check the labels more carefully when I'm at the grocery store. The lighting in there always gives me a headache anyway, so I just try to rush through... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the fidgeting. Yeah, I don't really think about doing it, my body just sort of does it on its own until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Despite all the fidgeting, difficulty focusing, and getting up from your seat, how are you actually doing with your work performance overall? Are you meeting your deadlines, getting good reviews, or is this stuff actually causing real problems for your job?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely starting to cause some issues. Like, it's creating friction, you know? At work and even at home. People are having to follow up with me a lot more than they used to. My manager had to ping me three times last week about a status report. And I hate that, because I don't want to be *that* person. I worry I'm becoming the unreliable one, especially when it comes to planning tasks or organizing things, even though I really am trying so hard. \n\nIt's just like with planning our family vacation this summer. I was supposed to book the Airbnb in Florida, but I kept putting it off because I couldn't decide which neighborhood was best, and then I started researching the history of Key West, which is actually super fascinating. Did you know Ernest Hemingway had six-toed cats there? And they still have descendants living at his house! I love cats. We have two, a tabby named Waffles and a black cat named Salem. Salem is a total menace, he keeps knocking my water glass off the nightstand. I had to buy a plastic tumbler just so I wouldn't have to keep vacuuming up broken glass at 3 AM. \n\nBut yeah, because I got distracted with all that, by the time I went to book the house, all the good ones were taken, and my husband was really frustrated. So, it's just... I'm trying, but the follow-ups and the dropped balls are definitely becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trouble focusing on work tasks and getting distracted by unrelated thoughts. Does this same difficulty with attention and mind-wandering happen in other areas of your life too—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with your husband, or doing things you actually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is everything, not just the boring stuff. Like, I can sit down to read our mortgage documents—we're actually looking into refinancing right now because the rates have been doing weird things. Honestly, I don't fully understand how all the interest rate stuff works. My brother-in-law is an accountant and he tried to explain it to me at Thanksgiving, but he talks so slowly. Thanksgiving was kind of a mess this year, by the way. I tried to deep-fry the turkey because I saw this video on YouTube about how it makes the skin super crispy, but we almost set the garage on fire. The fire extinguisher left this weird powdery residue on my husband's golf clubs that took forever to clean off. He hasn't even golfed in like three years, but he refuses to get rid of them. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the focus thing. So yeah, my mind just wanders off no matter what I'm trying to look at.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, I mean, it's always been there, right? Like I said about the teachers moving my desk. But honestly, it feels like it's gotten so much harder to manage lately. They made it to the state championships, which is great for them, I guess, but I was just standing there in the driveway freezing because it was November. I hate November weather. It's that weird in-between time where you don't know if you need a heavy coat or just a sweater, and I always guess wrong. I bought this really cute mustard yellow cardigan last fall, but it sheds everywhere. I wore it over a black shirt once and I looked like a bumblebee by the end of the day. 

Wait, sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, the mortgage. Yeah, so ever since we took all that on about two years ago, it's just been noticeably harder to keep a lid on the focus issues and the restlessness. It's like my brain just doesn't have the capacity for it all anymore.

Does this same difficulty with attention and mind-wandering happen in other areas of your life too—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with your husband, or doing things you actually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is everything, not just the boring stuff. Like, I can sit down to read our mortgage documents—we're actually looking into refinancing right now because the rates have been doing weird things. Honestly, I don't fully understand how all the interest rate stuff works. My brother-in-law is an accountant and he tried to explain it to me at Thanksgiving, but he talks so slowly. Thanksgiving was kind of a mess this year, by the way. I tried to deep-fry the turkey because I saw this video on YouTube about how it makes the skin super crispy, but we almost set the garage on fire. The fire extinguisher left this weird powdery residue on my husband's golf clubs that took forever to clean off. He hasn't even golfed in like three years, but he refuses to get rid of them. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the focus thing. Yeah, so I'll sit down with those mortgage documents, or even at work when I start reviewing a pull request from one of the junior devs. I always go in with really good intentions, you know? I tell myself I'm going to just knock it out. But then, after just a few minutes, I'll suddenly realize I’m just staring at the exact same section of text or the same block of code, and I'm not actually reading it at all. My brain is just completely gone, thinking about like five completely unrelated things at once—like if I remembered to buy cat food, or what happened to that one actor from that 90s sitcom, or if I should paint the guest bathroom green. So yeah, my mind just wanders off no matter what I'm trying to look at.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned teachers moving your desk around in elementary school and blurting out answers—so these attention and restlessness issues have been going on since you were a kid. But I want to be clear: were you having this same level of difficulty focusing and the constant need to move around back then as you're experiencing now, or has it gotten noticeably worse at some point in your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's always been there, right? Like I said about the teachers moving my desk. But honestly, it feels like it's gotten so much harder to manage lately. Really for about the last two years. That's when we bought the house and took on the mortgage, and suddenly there was just... so much extra paperwork and planning and just life admin that came with it. \n\nBuying a house is so stressful, by the way. The inspection alone was a nightmare. The guy found this tiny leak in the roof, and then we had to get a specialist out, and he was this older guy named Frank who just talked my ear off about his grandson's little league team. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, the focus stuff was definitely happening back then too, not just the moving around. Like, I completely remember my elementary school teachers having to move my desk away from my friends, or they'd constantly have to remind me to just stay in my seat because I was always popping up to look out the window or see what someone else was doing. And I was always blurting things out, talking before I was called on. I'd just rush through my worksheets, even when I totally understood the material, just to get it done. 

Which is kind of like how I cook now, honestly. I try to follow a recipe, but I get so impatient waiting for things to simmer or whatever, so I just turn the heat up to high to make it go faster. That's actually how I ruined my favorite non-stick pan last month. It was one of those ceramic ones, you know? Supposedly you can use metal utensils on them, but my husband used a metal spatula to make eggs and it totally scratched the bottom. He makes eggs every single morning. Just scrambled, nothing fancy. I don't get how someone can eat the exact same thing every day. I have to switch up my breakfast or I get so bored. Lately I've been doing overnight oats, but I keep forgetting to actually make them the night before, so then I just end up eating a handful of dry cereal in the car. 

But yeah, anyway, the elementary school stuff... it was all of it. The popping up, the talking out of turn, and rushing through my work. It's always been there, it just feels like the stakes are higher now, I guess.

Current symptoms include: persistent difficulty sustaining attention on tasks (mortgage documents, pull requests, meetings), internal restlessness/pressure to move despite appearing calm externally, constant fidgeting (pen clicking, knee bouncing, napkin shredding), frequent mind-wandering to unrelated topics, difficulty redirecting attention despite effort, chronic procrastination, organizational difficulties, and worsening over past 2 years with increased life demands. This presentation is consistent with ADHD, Combined Type (F90.1) or Predominantly Inattentive Type (F90.0), but given the prominent hyperactivity-impulsivity symptoms (constant movement, fidgeting, internal restlessness, blurting out answers as child) alongside inattention, F90.9 (ADHD, unspecified) is most appropriate pending formal testing. But then, maybe five minutes in, I'll realize I've just been staring at the exact same paragraph the whole time. \n\nMy brain will just be off somewhere else completely. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I remembered to switch the laundry, or if we need to buy more dog food, or... oh! I was actually thinking the other day about how I need to call my mom because her birthday is coming up next week. She's turning 68 and she really wants to go to this Italian place downtown, but parking there is just an absolute nightmare. Last time we went, we had to park like four blocks away in the pouring rain, and my husband ruined his favorite suede shoes. But anyway, yeah, I just... I start out fine, but then my mind is just suddenly juggling five completely unrelated things instead of looking at the code in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders like that during the pull request review, are you able to redirect yourself back to focus on the task if you really try, or does it feel almost impossible to pull your attention back no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: It's like... I try to pull myself back. I really do. I'll shake my head, blink a few times, and tell myself, \"Okay, focus on the code.\" And I can usually force myself to read the next line or two. But it's exhausting. It feels like trying to hold a beach ball underwater. Eventually, it just pops back up. \n\nSo yeah, I'll start with all these good intentions, totally meaning to get through the pull request or those mortgage papers, and then... boom. Five minutes later, I'm staring at the exact same section, completely zoned out, thinking about... I don't know, whether I should repaint the guest bathroom. We painted it this sort of seafoam green when we moved in, but now I'm thinking maybe a warm terracotta would be better? My sister just did her kitchen in terracotta and it looks amazing. She has all these copper pots hanging up, which I love, but honestly, who has the time to keep copper polished? I can barely keep up with the regular dishes. My husband is supposed to unload the dishwasher in the mornings, but he always leaves the Tupperware because he says it's still wet. Which, to be fair, it usually is. But anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Trying to focus. It's just really, really hard to keep my brain on track once it decides to wander off.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in those meetings or trying to focus on work, is the urge to get up and move around happening most of the time, or is it something that mainly kicks in when you're trying to concentrate on something boring or difficult?\n\nPatient: It's honestly... it's pretty much all the time. Like, even if I'm just sitting there, looking perfectly calm on the outside—like during one of those endless all-hands meetings we have on Thursdays. They cater lunch for those sometimes, which is nice. Last week they brought in sandwiches from that deli down on 4th Street, but they completely forgot the vegetarian options, so my coworker Sarah was so mad. She's been a strict vegetarian for like, ten years, ever since she took this trip to India. I've always wanted to go to India. The colors and the architecture just look incredible in pictures. \n\nBut yeah, during those meetings, even if I look fine, inside I feel this intense pressure. Like I absolutely need to move, or switch tasks, or just do something with my hands, or else I'm going to crawl out of my own skin. I usually end up shredding my napkins or clicking my pen until someone glares at me. I actually bought one of those fidget rings online to try and be more subtle about it. It was supposed to arrive yesterday, but the delivery guy left it at the wrong house. My neighbor down the street brought it over this morning—he's a retired pilot, always wears these really cool vintage aviator jackets... wait, sorry, what was the question again? Oh, the urge to move. Yeah, it's just this constant internal buzzing, really, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're fidgeting with things like the Rubik's cube or shredding napkins, does it feel like you're doing it to help yourself calm down or regulate your energy, or do you find yourself doing the same repetitive movements over and over again almost automatically, even if you're not really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It's... I think it's mostly automatic? Like, I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time. In meetings, I'm almost always bouncing my knee, or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair constantly. I really don't notice how much I'm doing it until someone looks over at me, you know? Like my manager, Dave. He has this very specific look he gives me when my pen clicking gets too loud. \n\nDave is actually leaving the company next month, which is a whole thing. He's moving to Austin. Everyone seems to be moving to Austin lately! My cousin moved there two years ago and she says the food scene is amazing, especially the tacos. I love a good breakfast taco. We tried to make them at home last weekend, but I accidentally bought corn tortillas instead of flour, and they just completely fell apart when I tried to fold them. My husband said it was fine and we could just eat them like a scramble, but it wasn't the same. I really need to remember to check the labels more carefully when I'm at the grocery store. The lighting in there always gives me a headache anyway, so I just try to rush through... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the fidgeting. Yeah, I don't really think about doing it, my body just sort of does it on its own until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Despite all the fidgeting, difficulty focusing, and getting up from your seat, how are you actually doing with your work performance overall? Are you meeting your deadlines, getting good reviews, or is this stuff actually causing real problems for your job?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely starting to cause some issues. Like, it's creating friction, you know? At work and even at home. People are having to follow up with me a lot more than they used to. My manager had to ping me three times last week about a status report. And I hate that, because I don't want to be *that* person. I worry I'm becoming the unreliable one, especially when it comes to planning tasks or organizing things, even though I really am trying so hard. \n\nIt's just like with planning our family vacation this summer. I was supposed to book the Airbnb in Florida, but I kept putting it off because I couldn't decide which neighborhood was best, and then I started researching the history of Key West, which is actually super fascinating. Did you know Ernest Hemingway had six-toed cats there? And they still have descendants living at his house! I love cats. We have two, a tabby named Waffles and a black cat named Salem. Salem is a total menace, he keeps knocking my water glass off the nightstand. I had to buy a plastic tumbler just so I wouldn't have to keep vacuuming up broken glass at 3 AM. \n\nBut yeah, because I got distracted with all that, by the time I went to book the house, all the good ones were taken, and my husband was really frustrated. So, it's just... I'm trying, but the follow-ups and the dropped balls are definitely becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trouble focusing on work tasks and getting distracted by unrelated thoughts. Does this same difficulty with attention and mind-wandering happen in other areas of your life too—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with your husband, or doing things you actually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is everything, not just the boring stuff. Like, I can sit down to read our mortgage documents—we're actually looking into refinancing right now because the rates have been doing weird things. Honestly, I don't fully understand how all the interest rate stuff works. My brother-in-law is an accountant and he tried to explain it to me at Thanksgiving, but he talks so slowly. Thanksgiving was kind of a mess this year, by the way. Like I said about the teachers moving my desk. But honestly, it feels like it's gotten so much harder to manage lately. Really for about the last two years. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Wait, didn't I just... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yeah, like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely happening back then. I clearly remember my teachers having to move my desk around all the time, or constantly reminding me to stay seated because I was always popping up out of my chair. I start out fine, but then my mind is just suddenly juggling five completely unrelated things instead of looking at the code in front of me.\n\nDoctor: When your mind wanders like that during the pull request review, are you able to redirect yourself back to focus on the task if you really try, or does it feel almost impossible to pull your attention back no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: It's like... I try to pull myself back. I really do. I'll shake my head, blink a few times, and tell myself, \"Okay, focus on the code.\" And I can usually force myself to read the next line or two. But it's exhausting. It feels like trying to hold a beach ball underwater. Eventually, it just pops back up. \n\nSo yeah, I'll start with all these good intentions, totally meaning to get through the pull request or those mortgage papers, and then... boom. Five minutes later, I'm staring at the exact same section, completely zoned out, thinking about... I don't know, whether I should repaint the guest bathroom. We painted it this sort of seafoam green when we moved in, but now I'm thinking maybe a warm terracotta would be better? My sister just did her kitchen in terracotta and it looks amazing. She has all these copper pots hanging up, which I love, but honestly, who has the time to keep copper polished? I can barely keep up with the regular dishes. My husband is supposed to unload the dishwasher in the mornings, but he always leaves the Tupperware because he says it's still wet. Which, to be fair, it usually is. But anyway... what was I saying? Oh, right. Trying to focus. It's just really, really hard to keep my brain on track once it decides to wander off.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in those meetings or trying to focus on work, is the urge to get up and move around happening most of the time, or is it something that mainly kicks in when you're trying to concentrate on something boring or difficult?\n\nPatient: It's honestly... it's pretty much all the time. Like, even if I'm just sitting there, looking perfectly calm on the outside—like during one of those endless all-hands meetings we have on Thursdays. They cater lunch for those sometimes, which is nice. Last week they brought in sandwiches from that deli down on 4th Street, but they completely forgot the vegetarian options, so my coworker Sarah was so mad. She's been a strict vegetarian for like, ten years, ever since she took this trip to India. I've always wanted to go to India. The colors and the architecture just look incredible in pictures. \n\nBut yeah, during those meetings, even if I look fine, inside I feel this intense pressure. Like I absolutely need to move, or switch tasks, or just do something with my hands, or else I'm going to crawl out of my own skin. I usually end up shredding my napkins or clicking my pen until someone glares at me. I actually bought one of those fidget rings online to try and be more subtle about it. It was supposed to arrive yesterday, but the delivery guy left it at the wrong house. My neighbor down the street brought it over this morning—he's a retired pilot, always wears these really cool vintage aviator jackets... wait, sorry, what was the question again? Oh, the urge to move. Yeah, it's just this constant internal buzzing, really, no matter what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're fidgeting with things like the Rubik's cube or shredding napkins, does it feel like you're doing it to help yourself calm down or regulate your energy, or do you find yourself doing the same repetitive movements over and over again almost automatically, even if you're not really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: It's... I think it's mostly automatic? Like, I don't even realize I'm doing it half the time. In meetings, I'm almost always bouncing my knee, or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair constantly. I really don't notice how much I'm doing it until someone looks over at me, you know? Like my manager, Dave. He has this very specific look he gives me when my pen clicking gets too loud. \n\nDave is actually leaving the company next month, which is a whole thing. He's moving to Austin. Everyone seems to be moving to Austin lately! My cousin moved there two years ago and she says the food scene is amazing, especially the tacos. I love a good breakfast taco. We tried to make them at home last weekend, but I accidentally bought corn tortillas instead of flour, and they just completely fell apart when I tried to fold them. My husband said it was fine and we could just eat them like a scramble, but it wasn't the same. I really need to remember to check the labels more carefully when I'm at the grocery store. The lighting in there always gives me a headache anyway, so I just try to rush through... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the fidgeting. Yeah, I don't really think about doing it, my body just sort of does it on its own until someone points it out.\n\nDoctor: Despite all the fidgeting, difficulty focusing, and getting up from your seat, how are you actually doing with your work performance overall? Are you meeting your deadlines, getting good reviews, or is this stuff actually causing real problems for your job?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's definitely starting to cause some issues. Like, it's creating friction, you know? At work and even at home. People are having to follow up with me a lot more than they used to. My manager had to ping me three times last week about a status report. And I hate that, because I don't want to be *that* person. I worry I'm becoming the unreliable one, especially when it comes to planning tasks or organizing things, even though I really am trying so hard. \n\nIt's just like with planning our family vacation this summer. I was supposed to book the Airbnb in Florida, but I kept putting it off because I couldn't decide which neighborhood was best, and then I started researching the history of Key West, which is actually super fascinating. Did you know Ernest Hemingway had six-toed cats there? And they still have descendants living at his house! I love cats. We have two, a tabby named Waffles and a black cat named Salem. Salem is a total menace, he keeps knocking my water glass off the nightstand. I had to buy a plastic tumbler just so I wouldn't have to keep vacuuming up broken glass at 3 AM. \n\nBut yeah, because I got distracted with all that, by the time I went to book the house, all the good ones were taken, and my husband was really frustrated. So, it's just... I'm trying, but the follow-ups and the dropped balls are definitely becoming a real problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trouble focusing on work tasks and getting distracted by unrelated thoughts. Does this same difficulty with attention and mind-wandering happen in other areas of your life too—like when you're reading for pleasure, watching a movie, having a conversation with your husband, or doing things you actually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really is everything, not just the boring stuff. Like, I can sit down to read our mortgage documents—we're actually looking into refinancing right now because the rates have been doing weird things. Honestly, I don't fully understand how all the interest rate stuff works. My brother-in-law is an accountant and he tried to explain it to me at Thanksgiving, but he talks so slowly. Thanksgiving was kind of a mess this year, by the way. I tried to deep-fry the turkey because I saw this video on YouTube about how it makes the skin super crispy, but we almost set the garage on fire. The fire extinguisher left this weird powdery residue on my husband's golf clubs that took forever to clean off. He hasn't even golfed in like three years, but he refuses to get rid of them. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the focus thing. Yeah, so I'll sit down with those mortgage documents, or even at work when I start reviewing a pull request from one of the junior devs. I always go in with really good intentions, you know? I tell myself I'm going to just knock it out. But then, after just a few minutes, I'll suddenly realize I’m just staring at the exact same section of text or the same block of code, and I'm not actually reading it at all. My brain is just completely gone, thinking about like five completely unrelated things at once—like if I remembered to buy cat food, or what happened to that one actor from that 90s sitcom, or if I should paint the guest bathroom green. So yeah, my mind just wanders off no matter what I'm trying to look at.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned teachers moving your desk around in elementary school and blurting out answers—so these attention and restlessness issues have been going on since you were a kid. But I want to be clear: were you having this same level of difficulty focusing and the constant need to move around back then as you're experiencing now, or has it gotten noticeably worse at some point in your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's always been there, right? Like I said about the teachers moving my desk. But honestly, it feels like it's gotten so much harder to manage lately. Really for about the last two years. That's when we bought the house and took on the mortgage, and suddenly there was just... so much extra paperwork and planning and just life admin that came with it. \n\nBuying a house is so stressful, by the way. The inspection alone was a nightmare. The guy found this tiny leak in the roof, and then we had to get a specialist out, and he was this older guy named Frank who just talked my ear off about his grandson's little league team. They made it to the state championships, which is great for them, I guess, but I was just standing there in the driveway freezing because it was November. I hate November weather. It's that weird in-between time where you don't know if you need a heavy coat or just a sweater, and I always guess wrong. I bought this really cute mustard yellow cardigan last fall, but it sheds everywhere. I wore it over a black shirt once and I looked like a bumblebee by the end of the day. \n\nWait, sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, the mortgage. Yeah, so ever since we took all that on about two years ago, it's just been noticeably harder to keep a lid on the focus issues and the restlessness. It's like my brain just doesn't have the capacity for it all anymore.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in elementary school—when your teachers were moving your desk around because you couldn't sit still—were you also having that same trouble focusing and keeping your mind on track back then, or did the attention problems mainly start showing up more recently in the last couple of years?\n\nPatient: Yeah, the focus stuff was definitely happening back then too, not just the moving around. Like, I completely remember my elementary school teachers having to move my desk away from my friends, or they'd constantly have to remind me to just stay in my seat because I was always popping up to look out the window or see what someone else was doing. And I was always blurting things out, talking before I was called on. I'd just rush through my worksheets, even when I totally understood the material, just to get it done. \n\nWhich is kind of like how I cook now, honestly. I try to follow a recipe, but I get so impatient waiting for things to simmer or whatever, so I just turn the heat up to high to make it go faster. That's actually how I ruined my favorite non-stick pan last month. It was one of those ceramic ones, you know? Supposedly you can use metal utensils on them, but my husband used a metal spatula to make eggs and it totally scratched the bottom. He makes eggs every single morning. Just scrambled, nothing fancy. I don't get how someone can eat the exact same thing every day. I have to switch up my breakfast or I get so bored. Lately I've been doing overnight oats, but I keep forgetting to actually make them the night before, so then I just end up eating a handful of dry cereal in the car. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the elementary school stuff... it was all of it. The popping up, the talking out of turn, and rushing through my work. It's always been there, it just feels like the stakes are higher now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to elementary school, do you remember having trouble paying attention and keeping your mind on your schoolwork even back then, or did those focus problems mainly start showing up later on?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't I just... I feel like we just talked about this, right? But yeah, like I was just saying a second ago, it was definitely happening back then. I clearly remember my teachers having to move my desk around all the time, or constantly reminding me to stay seated because I was always popping up out of my chair. And I was always blurting things out before I was called on, or just totally rushing through my work even when I understood exactly how to do it. I just wanted to get it over with. \n\nSpeaking of desks, do you remember those awful ones in elementary school where the chair is literally attached to the desk part by that metal bar? Those were so uncomfortable. My sister is left-handed, and she had the absolute worst time with those because the desk part was always on the right side. She actually just bought this beautiful vintage writing desk for her home office. She's an interior designer, so she's always finding these amazing pieces at estate sales and fixing them up. She just painted her kitchen cabinets this gorgeous dark navy blue, with all this brushed gold hardware. It looks so expensive, but she told me she got the handles off Amazon for like twenty bucks. I tried to buy the exact same ones for my guest bathroom, but they sent me the wrong size, and returning things on Amazon has gotten so annoying lately. You used to just be able to drop it off at Kohl's, but the Kohl's near us closed down last year. It turned into a Spirit Halloween for exactly two months, and now the building is just sitting there empty. I heard they might turn it into a gym, which would be nice, but who knows. \n\nBut um... wait, I lost my train of thought again. What were we just talking about? Oh, right, elementary school. But yeah, to answer your question, the focus stuff and the rushing and popping out of my seat, that was all definitely happening back when I was a kid, not just recently."
